#124. US midterm election results: Democrats win crucial seats. Here's what else the data tells us

Democrats have won the majority in the US Senate. House of Representatives votes are still being counted. DW takes a look at who voted Republican, who voted Democrat and what it all means.

We will update this article throughout the night as more data becomes available, so keep checking back. DW follows the Associated Press (AP) for when a seat is called for Republicans or Democrats.

In the 2026 midterms, Americans voted on all 435 members of the House of Representatives and 35 members of the Senate. Before election night, Republicans held the majority in both Congress chambers, if only by a hair.

Midterm elections are held every four years. The results will influence US politics for the next few years. Despite the significant executive power delegated to the presidency according to the US Constitution, the majority of federal political decisions have to be approved by Congress before they can be implemented.

For ongoing updates, go to our US midterms live blog.

In the US Senate, Democrats have managed to flip six Republican seats, winning races in North Carolina, South Carolina, Ohio, Kansas, Texas and Iowa. With that, they have won the majority of 51 seats required to gain control of the chamber.

In the House of Representatives, all 435 seats are up for election. 218 seats are required to build a majority. Democrats have so far flipped 13 seats, for a total of 187 seats so far. Republicans have retained 110 seats.

The data on voter demographics and attitudes is based on a representative survey of more than 75,000 verified voters conducted by survey and market research firm SSRS on behalf of AP and other news organizations. It builds on interviews of both early and mail-in voters as well as exit polls of in-person voters on Election Day. Where percentages of Republican and Democratic voters don't add up to 100%, the remaining percent were voters who voted for third-party candidates.

Republicans continue to make up the majority of white male voters. 55% of white men polled voted Republican. On the opposite end of the spectrum are Black women, 91% of whom voted Democrat. Remarkably, the majority of both Latino men and women voted Democrat in this election. Two years ago, Latinos were a big part of the vote that carried Trump to his second term in the White House.

Age-wise, Democrats are strongest among young voters. Three-quarters of 18- to 24-year-olds voted Democrat. On the flipside, Republicans lead with 51% among voters aged 65 and up.

The data shows that Republicans have lost support among all demographic groups compared to the last midterms in November 2022. Even among white men, the share of those who voted Republican fell from 60% to 55%. Among white women, the Republicans' share fell from 56% to 48%, and among Latinos, the percentage of Republican voters decreased as well.

The urban-rural divide that has been part of US politics for years continues to hold. Among urban voters, almost 70% voted Democrat, among suburban voters, that number stands at 57%. On the other hand almost 60% of rural and small town voters were Republicans.

Democrats make up 61% of voters with a college degree. Among voters without a college degree, the split is less significant: 45% voted Democrat and 51% Republican.

One of the most important factors in this year's midterms is the economy. Prices for gas have increased significantly since the start of the Iran war, and everyday goods have become more and more expensive across the country as well. Almost half of all Americans say their family's financial situation is worse than two years ago.

That sentiment is not the same across the board though. Among those saying their family is better off than two years ago, 75% voted Republican and 23% Democrat. Among those saying they are worse off, only 24% voted Republican and 72% Democrat. President Trump has been saying that the country is on the upswing.

On the question of which issue facing the country is most crucial, voters are divided among party lines. Of those who said immigration is the most important issue, 89% voted Republican and only 9% voted Democrat. Anti-immigrant rhetoric has been a mainstay of Trump's speeches. He promised to ramp up deportations at the start of his second term. Many states have seen a sharp increase in activity from federal immigration agencies. ICE (Immigration and Customs Enforcement) agents have been arresting immigrants off the street, even people who have ongoing asylum application processes, for example. Trump and his supporters say these actions are necessary to make the US safer.

Among those who say that the state of democracy is the most pressing issue in the US, 77% voted Democrat. Congressional Democrats and their supporters have been saying they are concerned about how Trump ignores direct judicial orders, for example to halt deportations, or Congressional resolutions. Among those saying the economy is the most important issue, there's an almost even split of Republican and Democratic voters.

American voters are split on major foreign policy decisions made by the Trump administration. Among those saying that the joint US-Israeli war against Iran was the right decision, 91% were Republican and only 7% Democratic voters. Among those saying it was the wrong decision to attack Iran, 80% were Democratic voters and 16% were Republican voters.

The US support for Israel has been a highly controversial topic ever since Israel began its strikes on Gaza in retaliation to the Hamas terror attack on Israel on October 7, 2023. The two countries have long been partners, with the US being the most important ally for Israel, and Jewish voters a crucial demographic in the US. But as the humanitarian situation in Gaza deteriorates, support for Israel among the US population, particularly among younger people and liberals, has been wavering. Of those saying US support for Israel is too strong, 77% were Democrat voters and 20% were Republican voters. Among those saying support is about right, only one quarter was Democrat voters.

Looking at how voters felt about the way the majority-Republican Congress did its job over the last two years, there's something of a surprise. Among those dissatisfied with the House's and Senate's work, one third are Republican voters. With the extreme level of polarization in the US, reaching compromises to pass laws or approve a budget in Congress has become harder in recent years. That could be contributing to dissatisfaction.

Of people seeing their country as going in the right direction in general, only 4% were Democrat voters. Of those saying they're dissatisfied or even angry about the US's current direction, 69% were Democrat voters — and 27% Republican, meaning they are unhappy with their own leaders.

Unsurprisingly, 91% of those who said they approved of ICE's actions were Republican, while of those who disapproved, 85% were Democrat. Regarding Trump's tariff policy, there's a split along party lines as well. The president had introduced sharply  increased tariffs on goods from a large number of US trading partners. Trump said the move was intended to even out unfair trade balances and protect American manufacturing. He has also used tariffs to punish countries he saw as acting against his political interests. The trade measures have made many goods more expensive for Americans.

Trump is not on the ballot, but the midterms are considered a referendum on how he and his Republicans governed the country for the past two years. Most recently, Trump's approval ratings have fallen to the lowest he has ever had, with just under 30% of Americans saying they think he is doing a good job.

In recent history, only George W. Bush has fared worse, with 25% at the end of his second term. During the 2006 midterm election, Bush's Republican Party lost control of both chambers of Congress.

Exit polls showed that half of voters saw the 2026 midterm elections as an opportunity to rebuke the president. Three-quarters of US voters said they are "angry" or "dissatisfied" with the way the country is headed.

According to the AP Voter Poll, dissatisfaction was very high in Democratic-leaning states such as California and Massachusetts. However, even in Republican-leaning states, a majority of voters were at least "dissatisfied."

Before Tuesday's election, Republicans (and independents who caucus with them) held 53 of 100 seats in the Senate and 220 of 435 seats in the House. That means they only held two more seats than the 218 required for a majority in the House and only two more than the 51 majority required in the Senate. In recent years, such razor-thin majorities have been the lay of the land, especially in the House of Representatives.

A party doesn’t flip both the House and Senate very often. Since World War II, both chambers have flipped from one party to the other in the same midterm just three times: 1946, 1994 and 2006.

#122. US strikes Iran’s Qeshm Island as Vance voices optimism about a deal

Strikes on eve of US midterms reportedly target surface-to-air missiles being used against US assets

The ⁠US launched limited strikes on ​Iran’s Qeshm Island late on Monday, hours after the vice-president, JD Vance, said he was optimistic that diplomatic efforts to end the war were moving in the right direction.

The strikes, disclosed by Iranians on social media and confirmed by US Central Command (Centcom), came on the eve of crucial midterm elections in which Donald Trump’s Republican party is expected to be punished by voters. Diplomatic mediators believe it will be clear within two days whether the basis for continued indirect talks between the US and Iran exists.

The US tried to downplay the significance of the attacks by stressing they were limited and not the precursor of a much-anticipated major assault on Iran that Trump has promised after the midterms if the US adjudges Iran’s nuclear program continues as a threat to the west.

The US described the strikes as defensive in nature, aimed at missile batteries from which Iranian attacks on US planes had been mounted the day before.

Inside Iran, blame was initially placed on Israel, but later it was conceded that the US was responsible. Israel itself insisted it had no role in the attacks. Similar military exchanges have happened at least three times since the cessation of major hostilities, as the US and Iran jostle for control of the strait.

Iran’s deputy foreign minister, Kazem Gharibabadi, referring to the overnight US strikes, said: “We are still in a state of war. The aggression against Iran has not ended in our view, and until the military aggression is completely and permanently terminated Iran has the right to legitimate defence.”

He added Iran was not waiting for the results from the midterms to adjust its negotiating position, saying: “We do not tie our foreign policies to the elections of other countries.”

Iran would like to see Trump weakened in those elections, but recognises the two-month interlude between the elections and the start of the newly elected Congress represents the point of maximum tension.

Chief negotiator Mohammad Ghalibaf made no direct mention of the attacks during an address to parliament, but said any miscalculation by the US would lead to an unpredictable Iranian response. In common with other Iranian leaders, he suggested the attacks would extend to US bases in Europe. Maps of Nato bases in Europe have appeared on the front page of the more conservative newspapers, implying they will be targets in any war.

The greater concern in Tehran appears to be the apparent inability of Qatari mediators to bridge the gap between Iran and the US over the upfront commitments Iran must make to end its nuclear program irreversibly, a phrase used by JD Vance.

It was Vance, in a letter to Mohsen Rezaei, the secretary of the supreme national security council, who proposed the restart of talks, but so far those talks have not led to any direct contact between the US and Iranian sides.

On Tuesday Rezaei appeared to reject the US response as inadequate, saying on X: “America’s response to Iran’s rights and demands was not convincing, and they must fully and explicitly address Iran’s seven conditions and act upon them; only in this case will the Strait of Hormuz be reopened.” The seven conditions include the unfreezing of Iran assets abroad, and the lifting of sanctions on oil exports.

Iran has offered to lift the blockade on the strait of Hormuz within two days of the US lifting economic constraints on Iran. Once those two days, elapse Iran would also enter into talks about its nuclear program, but Tehran has refused to give upfront concessions either about the return of UN inspectors to visit Iran’s bombed nuclear sites or to agree to ship the 400kg of highly enriched uranium to a third country. The most likely compromise is a down-blending of the stockpile inside Iran under international supervision, and with an external guarantor, such as China.

Esmail Baghaei, the foreign ministry spokesman, struck a pessimistic note saying the US attitude to Iran’s nuclear program was “the biggest and most costly lie of the century”, and was fundamentally based on having a problem with Iran as an idea and historical entity.

Qatar said its mediation was continuing, and there was a very high level of coordination by countries in the region. Most of these Gulf states know they will be attacked by Iran if the US mounts an assault, and are desperate to avoid further damage to their fraying economies. Majed al-Ansari, the spokesman for the Qatar foreign ministry, insisted both sides remained committed to the process.

Speaking to Fox News hours before the polls opened in the US on Tuesday, Trump continued to play down the significance of the conflict and its effect on the US economy.

“There’s no war raging. It could happen if they don’t make a deal, but they want to make a deal very badly and there’s a very good chance we make a deal.”

However, he said the US might still “have to go after Pickaxe Mountain” – referring to a fortified underground site in central Iran that is suspected to be a nuclear facility – but said it would not be a “big deal” and could be done in “one hour”.

Speaking from Washington in his closing message of the midterms campaign, Trump hailed a sharp drop in oil prices, as his administration touted renewed diplomatic efforts being made to end the war. Rising petrol prices caused by the war have become a signature issue of the midterm elections.

#120. If hackers can take control of a supermarket freezer, how vulnerable is other tech?

Supermarkets across Europe have been hit by a suspected cyber-attack affecting their fridges and freezers, and raising concern about the vulnerabilities of internet-connected hardware from industrial machinery to humble domestic appliances.

Smart devices which are connected to the internet can include fridges, central heating, and even washing machines.

And while that makes them easier to control remotely, in theory, any device which goes online can potentially be exploited by malicious actors.

However, that doesn't mean hackers can easily take control of it, and big brands generally go to great lengths to make sure their tech is secure.

Exploits have been found before in some devices. In 2015, researchers demonstrated how a vulnerability in a Samsung smart fridge could allow them to intercept internet traffic.

But there's no evidence domestic appliances are being targeted in the cyber-attacks which targeted supermarkets in November.

Ciaran Martin, former chief executive of the UK's National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC) says manufacturers needed to do more to make connected devices secure.

"There's a lot more that we need to demand of big tech to give us safer devices and products in the first place," he said.

He advised consumers to keep devices updated and enable multi-factor authentication wherever possible.

"I know it's a pain, but it only takes a few seconds," he said.

Since April 2024, UK law has required manufacturers of certain consumer smart devices to meet minimum cyber security standards, including requirements around passwords and security updates.

The NCSC said it was working with international partners and industry to understand the supermarket incidents, but it was unaware of any impact on the UK.

However, researchers from UK Cyber Defence have identified more than 800 refrigeration and building-control interfaces associated with British retailers that can be accessed over the public internet.

That doesn't necessarily mean they can be hacked, but it could leave them exposed to attackers exploiting weak passwords or software vulnerabilities.

The government has already held talks with senior food retailers about cyber resilience, following earlier attacks on supermarkets.

Helen Dickinson, chief executive of the British Retail Consortium, has said cyber incidents are a major concern for retailers, with businesses working alongside the NCSC to share intelligence and strengthen their defences.

It would not be the first time that hacks have raised concern about impact on food supply.

When UK supermarkets M&S and the Co-op were targeted by ransomware hackers in May 2025, the retailers struggled to stock some shop shelves with fresh deliveries.

Customers across the UK complained of empty shelves in the days following the attack.

Tom Hegel, a researcher at cyber-security firm SentinelOne, said retailers must be clearer about responsibility.

"The question retailers need to answer is who owns the security of that equipment, not just who repairs it," he said.

He said internet connectivity can make everyday technology more convenient, but security needed to keep pace.

"Once an attacker can operate those controls, legitimate functions such as defrosting become tools for disruption," he said.

"Changing a setting can destroy physical stock, not just interrupt a computer system."

Outside of the home, connected tech is used to remotely manage industrial machinery and vehicles.

Cyber security expert Ken Munro pointed to a 2015 demonstration in which researchers remotely hacked a Jeep through its internet-connected entertainment system, prompting improvements in vehicle security.

"Fortunately, the vehicle industry woke up to this and fixed the problem," he said.

Mobile connections are also widely used to monitor equipment in power plants and water networks, allowing engineers to manage systems remotely.

Munro said UK utilities were generally good at securing these connections, but standards varied around the world.

His biggest concern was smaller, poorly funded utility companies which may still be relying on older, vulnerable equipment.

Without proper protections, "any factory or equipment" connected to the internet - ranging from carmakers to electricity or transport infrastructure - could face similar risks, said Olivier Daloy, a cybersecurity expert at Zscaler.

#119. Trump claims US will end military aid to Israel by end of this year

US president makes surprise election day announcement, saying country does not ‘need’ $4bn annual payment

Donald Trump has said that the US will end military aid to Israel at the end of this year in a surprise election day announcement that appeared to be targeted at voters sceptical of US support for Israel and the war in Gaza.

In a phone interview with Fox News as voting began on Tuesday morning, the US president said the US would cut nearly $4bn (£2.96bn) in military aid to Israel because “they don’t need it”.

“We were giving them $4bn … But it gets cut off at the end of the year,” he said.

Trump also indicated that he had decided to end the aid – which he claimed would be replaced by arms sales to Israel instead – because it had become a popular criticism of US foreign policy led by outspoken candidates including the Michigan Democrat Abdul El-Sayed.

“Why should we suffer the slings from lunatics like El-Sayed when they don’t need it?” Trump said. “It’s a bad talking point.”

During the interview, Trump claimed that the abrupt ending of aid was proposed by Israel’s prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu. “We give them $4bn of things and money, and they don’t need it,” he said. “And I was approached by Bibi Netanyahu, and we’ve been talking about it for a couple of years because it’s a lot of money.”

Netanyahu had previously suggested tapering off US military aid to Israel over the next decade, but Trump’s announcement on Tuesday would significantly accelerate that timeline.

Israeli officials appeared to be taken by surprise by the announcement, and leading members of the opposition said it showed the damage that Netanyahu had done to the US-Israel relationship. The announcement comes at a crucial moment after Netanyahu’s party lost its majority in the Knesset in last week’s elections and the opposition is trying to cobble together a coalition to replace him.

“The fact that the security establishment, along with Israeli citizens, is learning about a change in the strategic relationship framework through the media highlights just how much Benjamin Netanyahu’s conduct poses a genuine danger,” said Gadi Eisenkot, a centre-right rival to Netanyahu who is seeking to replace him as prime minister. “We can no longer rely on him and must replace him.”

“A responsible prime minister would have fought to increase American aid, not dismantle it,” wrote Yair Golan, the head of the Democrats opposition party. “Netanyahu is deepening the security and economic crisis, putting Israel’s future at risk.”

Israel received most of the approximately $3.8bn in 2025 as foreign military financing grants that allow the country to buy US military hardware including F-35 fighter jets. Another $500m was given for joint missile defence programmes.

Get the most important US headlines and highlights emailed direct to you every morning

Trump’s announcement on Tuesday appeared timed to affect the outcome of the midterm elections, in which US foreign policy and support for Israel have played an unusually important role in key races, including the Michigan senatorial race where El-Sayed is running against Republican Mike Rogers. El-Sayed would become the country’s first Muslim senator if he is elected.

A Quinnipiac University poll released in September showed that 48% of Americans believed that the US was too supportive of Israel, and other recent polls have shown a historic shift away from US support for Israel and Netanyahu’s government.

Anger over the humanitarian crisis in Gaza has helped usher in a wave of progressive Democratic candidates including El-Sayed, and Trump’s vice-president, JD Vance, has openly accused Israel of trying to sabotage US negotiations with Iran. In a June interview with the New York Times, Vance said of Israel’s foreign policy: “You can’t just kill your way out of solving every single national security problem that you have.”

During his Fox News interview, Trump spoke warmly about Netanyahu and also boasted about his own popularity in Israel. “If people ever got tired of me in the US, I’ll move to Israel and become prime minister,” he said.

#117. ANC under pressure to reverse South Africa decline

More than 500 parties are contesting the elections

South Africans are voting in highly contested local elections likely to see a haemorrhaging of votes for the country's former ruling party, the African National Congress (ANC).

This year's voting season has been marked by intense campaigns that have included politicians spinning cars, watching grannies knit and even wading through giant-sized potholes.

Leaders are campaigning to head South Africa's nearly 300 municipalities, responsible for basic service provision such as water, electricity and refuse collection.

For the ANC, the elections are a chance to prove it still has what it takes to govern. The party had a disastrous showing in the last national election, when it lost its parliamentary majority for the first time since 1994 when the racist apartheid system ended.

Support for the party has been dropping significantly over the years because of anger over high levels of corruption, unemployment and crime.

At its peak, in 2004, the ANC got 70% of the vote in the general election. Two decades later its share dropped to 40%.

Polls and pundits have predicted a further loss in these elections but President Cyril Ramaphosa has denied that his party was in a downward spiral.

"What I'm seeing here is that the ANC is going higher and higher and on the 4th [of November] we will show those naysayers," he told supporters at the party's final election rally on Sunday.

In townships like Alexandra, north-east of Johannesburg, the ANC has traditionally had a strong supporter base but poor governance and voter apathy are unravelling this support.

The BBC visited the sprawling township a week before the elections and spoke to residents to get their views. One of them was Sanele Ntombela, who is a first-time voter excited to make his voice heard.

Sanele Ntombela (left), seen here with his friends Tshiamo Mbange (centre) and Lwandle Dladla, is excited to cast his vote

We found the 18-year-old hanging out with his friends on a street corner. Most of his friends said they had no intention of voting and had not registered to do so.

They cited a lack of change and deteriorating delivery of basic services.

But Ntombela was looking forward to voting for the first time and thought it might lead to change.

"We face many challenges in Alexandra. There's rubbish everywhere and we struggle with electricity and water and many other issues," he said.

Just metres away, heaps of rubbish lay gathered beneath a streetlight pole covered in election posters from the different political parties.

On the other side of the street, drain water trickles through the many potholes that have gutted that part of the road.

The ANC faces immense pressure to reverse years of decline

The BBC also spoke to Zandile Mncube, who said she wouldn't vote, even though she is registered to do so.

The 33-year-old woman also cited poor service delivery and performance by local leaders for her decision.

"It makes no difference [whether I vote or not]. Things have gotten worse over the years," she said.

Alex, as the township is known, is a stone's throw away from Sandton, Africa's richest square mile, but the differences could not be starker.

Sandton is known for its skyscrapers, designer shops and well-maintained roads but in Alex, houses are sandwiched together and rubbish, either piled together or left strewn across the pavement, is everywhere. The stench of raw sewage and uncollected rubbish is heavy in the air, but residents appear used to it.

The issues plaguing Alexandra are not unique. A drive around Gauteng province reveals similar patterns of neglect and disrepair, especially in the townships.

South of Johannesburg, Soweto looks marginally cleaner than Alexandra, but residents also complain about service delivery.

Mduduzi Makhubo, 45, has lived in Soweto his entire life and he told the BBC of the community's daily struggles with accessing water and electricity.

"Every Monday we know we won't have water after 2pm," he said.

Makhubo is registered to vote and intends to use his vote to deal with the "arrogance" he says local leaders have towards residents and "change the status quo".

Can this man broaden the appeal of a South African party seen by some as 'too white'?

South Africa's political marriage of convenience avoids divorce - just

These areas offer a small, but important, glimpse into the state of South Africa's local governance.

Years of reports by the auditor-general have highlighted the dire state of most of South Africa's 257 municipalities.

A recent report only gave 39 clean audits - the highest ranking a public entity can achieve - between 2024 and 2025. None of Gauteng's three metro cities, Johannesburg, Tshwane and Ekurhuleni, featured in this list.

Earlier this year auditor-general Tsakani Maluleke said Johannesburg "needs ongoing attention".

"If we don't that, we are going to continue on this slide where financial health deteriorates [and] where service delivery continues to be compromised, which is what we are living," she told lawmakers.

The city has been labelled "effectively bankrupt" due to the dire state of its finances. Dirty streets, unstable electricity supply and persistent water issues have been common for years.

Experts the BBC spoke to agreed that all eyes will be on Africa's wealthiest city as well as the country's seven other biggest municipalities, or metros, particularly those in Gauteng when the results come in.

Of South Africa's eight metros, only three are governed by a single party. In Gauteng, all three metros are governed by coalition governments, with the ANC the largest party.

Political analyst Melanie Verwoerd said the elections would be a "verdict ... and judgment" on the ANC.

"And the expectation is that the ANC generally in the country is going to [perform] the worst it's ever done," she said.

Several polls point to a continued decline in support for the party in Wednesday's election, contested by over 500 political parties.

One, conducted by Ipsos, put the ANC's support at 30%, down from the 46% it got at the last local elections in 2021.

The same poll, along with others, points to a slight increase in electoral support for the Democratic Alliance (DA), especially in Johannesburg. Ipsos predicts the DA will garner 25% support in this election, up from 22% last time.

DA leader Helen Zille has been vying for the mayoral chain in the city and her unusual campaign has attracted a lot of attention.

The former Cape Town mayor has been captured swimming in crater-sized potholes, ziplining over sinkholes and riding into her final rally in the city in a classic BMW 325i, known locally as a "Gusheshe".

She believes the DA is the country's best choice for a government, telling local radio broadcaster 702 last week that "towns and cities that work best are under DA governance".

Verwoerd, a former ANC MP and South Africa's ambassador to Ireland, predicted that coalitions would continue being the order of the day across Gauteng.

These types of arrangements have often been viewed as unstable and chaotic at a local level and the constant change in leadership, usually caused by parties falling out with each other, has affected service delivery in many municipalities.

Zandile Mncube says things have got worse in her township over the years

Verwoerd said in her view, the most stable coalitions would be those that replicate what the ANC and DA have done on a national level, where the historic rivals have agreed to share power.

Reflecting on Cape Town, she said it was unlikely that the DA would lose control of the municipality. The coastal city is widely considered the country's best-performing metro and has been run by the DA since the late 2000s when it unseated the ANC.

Ethekwini, which includes the port city of Durban in KwaZulu-Natal, would be a "different story", Verwoerd said.

The uMkhonto weSizwe (MK) Party has been projected to perform well in the area but it is unclear if it will secure the outright majority needed to run the metro. Polls suggest it won't and will need partners to form a government.

The party was founded by former South African President Jacob Zuma and was the biggest party across his home province of KwaZulu-Natal in the 2024 provincial election.

Verwoerd reflected that while various options were on the table for the party, forming a government would be "very messy" given its refusal to work with either the ANC or DA.

A confident Zuma, addressing supporters on Sunday, vowed to take control of a number of municipalities after the elections.

Another political analyst Dr Levy Ndou echoed Verwoerd's assessment, saying that a coalition between the DA and ANC seemed unavoidable in most metros.

"We're not sure whether the arrangement of the government of national unity could have the potential to influence how the metros are going to be run," he said.

Both experts also spoke about the declining voter turnout in South Africa's elections. Over 29 million citizens are registered to vote, but the turnout, especially among young people, has seen a steady decline.

Ndou attributed this to the "calibre" of the candidates campaigning and the lack of visibility of local councillors, who are often the ones elected to lead their neighbourhoods, in election campaigns.

South Africa's electoral commission has seven days to announce the results, but parties have 14 days to form a governing structure.

#116. Seven planes across Europe searched or diverted after ‘malicious hoax’ bomb threats

Unclear if series of incidents affecting two UK departures and several Ryanair services, among others, were connected

Seven planes across Europe have been searched or diverted after a series of bomb threats were made to passenger flights, prompting questions as to whether there was any coordination behind the warnings.

Two UK departures – one from Heathrow and one from Manchester – were among those threatened in what Downing Street called “malicious hoaxes”, though all planes landed safely and no explosives were found.

A UK Department for Transport source added that the low-level threats were “not credible” and that passengers should not be concerned, though the warnings disrupted flights and prompted investigations across the UK and Europe.

None of the incidents and subsequent searches threw up any evidence of terrorist activity, though authorities were responding to an unusual number of reported threats in one day.

One of Warsaw’s two main airports, Modlin, was briefly closed after receiving a diverted plane, and Polish officials confirmed that they had received bomb threats involving two flights coming into the capital.

Italian fighter jets escorted a Ryanair plane into Rome following a warning of a suspected threat on a flight coming from Dublin. The flight landed safely and nothing untoward was reported as being found.

There was little obvious pattern surrounding the incidents, which affected flights taking off from the UK, Ireland, Denmark, Portugal and Italy, and landing in Poland, Spain, Italy and Bulgaria. Four of the flights were operated by Ryanair, the others by Wizz Air, LOT and SAS.

Investigators added there was no immediate evidence of a hostile state being behind the threats, though they continued to monitor developments, sharing information across the continent while inquiries continued.

Aviation security fears have been reignited with the recent FlyDubai incident when a co-pilot allegedly attempted to crash a Tel Aviv-bound plane. Russia has also been accused of being behind firebomb threats targeting parcel carriers using cargo planes over the past two years.

The threats followed an incident on Monday when another flight from the UK to Bulgaria was diverted, apparently without substance.

Experts said that fake threats occur on a daily basis. Philip Baum, visiting professor of aviation security at Coventry University, said: “Most are from individuals who think it is funny or want to delay a flight for some reason. If the industry was to respond to every unsubstantiated threat, flights would grind to a halt.

“The reality is that terrorist organisations who might like to smuggle a bomb on to a flight don’t tell people, they claim responsibility after the attacks.”

But he added that the fact that some aircraft did divert would “imply that the threats are credible and specific in nature, and therefore one has to respond accordingly”.

Tuesday’s series of incidents started with a flight from Heathrow operated by the Polish carrier LOT, which was scheduled to arrive at Warsaw’s Chopin airport but was diverted to the second airport, further from the Polish capital, Modlin. Authorities closed that airport after the alert and the Boeing 737 Max landed without incident.

“The passengers were evacuated and the plane underwent security checks. Nothing was found,” spokesperson Adam Szablowski told AFP. “The airport has been reopened and is operating normally.”

A Scandinavian Airlines plane from Copenhagen to Warsaw was also the subject of a threat but landed at Chopin airport, where a security inspection that did not turn up any explosive devices closed part of the airport.

Another UK departure, a Ryanair plane from Manchester, was met by emergency vehicles on landing at its destination, Murcia in southern Spain, after another threat. Spanish regional authorities confirmed on social media that no explosives were found on the flight.

The Metropolitan police confirmed its aviation unit is monitoring the situation, adding no security issues were identified following threats made against flights after they had taken off.

A UK government spokesperson said of the threats to two departures from Britain: “The UK has one of the most robust aviation security systems in the world, with well-tested processes for dealing with any potential threats, as well as malicious hoax threats, like those we’ve seen today.

“Our world-leading police and intelligence agencies work closely every day to keep us safe, using the tools they have to assess and respond to potential threats.”

In Italy, two Ryanair flights were searched at Rome’s Fiumicino airport following potential bomb threats, according to the airport operator ADR. The first was a flight from Lisbon, while a second later in the morning from Dublin was escorted in by Italian air force fighter jets until landing.

Bulgaria’s ​transport ‌ministry said ‌in a statement to ‌Reuters that threats were ​made against a Wizz Air flight ⁠from Bergamo and a Ryanair flight from Dublin to ⁠the capital, Sofia. Both flights were carrying about 180 passengers and landed safely, with no devices found.

The separate incidents came the day after a Wizz Air service from London Luton made an emergency landing in Budapest, escorted by a Hungarian fighter jet.

The plane, which was due to depart from Luton at 8.15am on Monday for Sofia, was diverted after a bomb threat was received “during the flight”, Wizz Air said.

Airlines abide by a regulated process to assess and manage bomb threats, although virtually all are fake. In the UK, airlines file a mandatory occurrence report to the Civil Aviation Authority.

Ryanair did not respond to requests for comment.

#113. Seven reported bomb threats against European flights confirmed as false alarms – as it happened

Bomb threats were made on routes involving a number of airlines, including Ryanair and WizzAir

Bringing it all together, seven bomb threats have now been reported in total:

LOT Polish Airlines flight from London Heathrow, UK to Warsaw Okęcie, diverted to Warsaw Modlin, Poland (10:33, 10:58, 12:00, 13:57).

SAS flight from Copenhagen, Denmark to Warsaw Okęcie (11:33, 12:59).

Ryanair flight from Manchester, UK to Murcia, Spain (12:44, 13:44)

Ryanair flight from Lisbon, Portugal to Rome, Italy (15:02)

Ryanair flight from Dublin, Ireland to Rome, Italy

WizzAir flight from Bergamo, Italy to Sofia, Bulgaria (15:41)

Ryanair flight from Dublin, Ireland to Sofia, Bulgaria

All seven have been confirmed to be false alarms.

At least seven flights and a cruise ship in Europe received bomb threats, causing disruption at airports although ⁠no devices were discovered. At least two flights to Warsaw, two to Sofia, two to Rome and one to Murcia airport in Spain were subjected to the threats on Tuesday and another flight to Sofia had been threatened a day earlier. ⁠

An airport in Warsaw was briefly ⁠shut and the Italian Air Force ​accompanied one of the flights to Rome, while an Israel-bound cruise ship was searched for bombs in Greece.

Authorities diverted a LOT flight from London that was meant to land at Warsaw’s Chopin airport to nearby Modlin airport, which was closed and then reopened ​after the plane was searched and no device was found. Another Warsaw-bound flight, an ‌SAS flight from Copenhagen, landed at Chopin as ‌scheduled. Chopin airport said no device was found and the airport remained operational.

Bomb threats were made against planes traveling from Milan/Bergamo and Dublin ‌to Sofia, Bulgaria’s transport ministry said in a statement to Reuters. Both landed safely and no explosive devices were found on inspection.

The Bergamo flight was operated by Wizz Air and was carrying 176 passengers, the statement said. The Dublin flight, operated by Ryanair, had 186 passengers on board. Bulgarian news agency BTA had earlier quoted an interior ministry official as saying a flight from Israel had been affected, but authorities later said this was not the case.

In Murcia in southeastern ‌Spain, parts of an airport were cordoned off after a bomb threat was reported on a Ryanair flight from Manchester. Officials said the report proved to be a hoax, and the airport resumed normal operations after authorities found no explosives on board.

Two other Ryanair flights - one from Lisbon and another from Dublin - were searched at Rome’s Fiumicino airport following bomb threats, said a source from airport operator ADR. The Dublin flight was escorted by the Italian Air Force until it landed. The alert was cleared and the airport continued to operate as usual with no effects on other flights, the person said.

An explosives detection dog and a diver searched the Panama-flagged Crown Iris cruise ship on the Greek island of Syros after an unknown person said an explosive device had ⁠been attached to the boat’s hull, authorities said. Initial checks found no explosive device with coast guard specialists expected to carry ​out further inspections before the ship could resume its voyage.

Meanwhile, Czech public media workers went on a one-day strike to protest a government plan to overhaul how public broadcasters are funded. The legislation, led by populist billionaire turned prime minister Andrej Babiš, has been criticised as threatening the independence of the media organisations.

Russia bombarded Ukraine’s energy infrastructure in waves on ⁠Tuesday, knocking out power to swathes of the country with dozens of missiles and hundreds of drones, a week before US-brokered peace talks were due to resume.

At a ​press conference in Berlin with German chancellor Friedrich Merz, Volodymyr Zelenskyy said the attack showed that ‌Moscow was intent on killing people instead of building ‌on the humanitarian ceasefire in and around Oleshky.

Merz warned that the outcome of the war in Ukraine “will determine European security for a long time to come.” He said Germany will be coordinating with France and the UK, and will host a meeting of the Coalition of the Willing in Berlin later this month.

The European Commission will host ⁠a meeting regarding Ukraine with representatives ⁠from the ⁠United ​States, Ukraine and its key European allies on Wednesday, Commission ⁠spokesperson Paula Pinho said. “So tomorrow the European ⁠Commission will host a ​meeting with representatives ‌from Ukraine, ‌from the United States, France, Germany, ‌the UK, Nato and the World Bank and this meeting will build on earlier talks that took place ‌in Miami,” Pinho told reporters in Brussels.

A bomb threat to an ⁠Israel-bound cruise ship prompted a security operation at the ⁠Greek island port of ⁠Syros after an unknown person claimed ⁠an explosive device had been attached to its hull, Greek authorities said.

The ⁠Panama-flagged Crown Iris was due ​to sail ‌to Haifa carrying ‌940 passengers and 597 crew ‌members when the threat was received, Reuters reported.

Coast guard personnel assisted by an explosives detection dog and a private diver searched the vessel, while ‌a special coast guard unit was deployed to the scene.

Initial ​checks found no explosive device. Coast guard specialists are expected to arrive at 9.30pm (7.30pm GMT) ⁠to carry out further inspections before the ​ship ​can resume its voyage.

Here are some images from earlier today as Czech public media workers went on a one-day strike to protest a government plan to overhaul how public broadcasters are funded.

The legislation, led by populist billionaire turned prime minister Andrej Babiš, has been criticised as threatening the independence of the media organisations. The plan led to street protests in Prague last month.

Under the plan, the country’s public radio and television would be financed from the state budget, not from an existing system of regular fees from individuals, households and businesses. The media would receive about 15% less money than they do now, which could likely lead to staff layoffs, limited production and program cancellations.

Critics say the overhaul would give the ruling three-party coalition the means to take control of the media, similar to what populist governments did in Slovakia under prime minister Robert Fico and in Hungary before former prime minister Viktor Orbán was ousted from power in April.

Michal Kubal, chief news editor at Czech Television, said the strike today is unprecedented in the history of Czech public service media.

On their TV screens, Czech residents on Tuesday saw an announcement saying:

double quotation markWe are doing everything we can to ensure that we can continue to provide a public service in its full scope and quality in the future.

The UK government confirmed that two of the planes affected had departed from British airports.

double quotation markThe UK has one of the most robust aviation security systems in the world, with well-tested processes for dealing with any potential threats, as well as malicious hoax threats, like those we’ve seen today.

Our world-leading police and intelligence agencies work closely every day to keep us safe, using the tools they have to assess and respond to potential threats.

It’s also worth recalling some other similar incidents in the last few weeks.

On 18 October, an Austrian Airlines flight from Tel Aviv to ​Vienna diverted to Sofia ‌after a reported bomb ​threat while over Romania, Reuters said.

And yesterday, on 2 November, a WizzAir flight from London to Sofia was diverted to Budapest after a bomb threat (10:44).

Reuters is carrying a quick confirmation that the flight from Bergamo to Sofia in Bulgaria was operated by WizzAir, not Ryanair.

This has also been updated in the tally at the top of the page.

Bringing it all together, seven bomb threats have now been reported in total:

LOT Polish Airlines flight from London Heathrow, UK to Warsaw Okęcie, diverted to Warsaw Modlin, Poland (10:33, 10:58, 12:00, 13:57).

SAS flight from Copenhagen, Denmark to Warsaw Okęcie (11:33, 12:59).

Ryanair flight from Manchester, UK to Murcia, Spain (12:44, 13:44)

All seven have been confirmed to be false alarms.

There is also an update from Bulgaria, with the country’s transport ministry telling Reuters the bomb threats were actually made against two planes from Bergamo in Italy and Dublin in Ireland, and not from Israel as previously reported.

That brings the total to seven planes (that we know of).

Back to Berlin, Ukraine’s Zelenskyy also rejects Russia’s suggestion of a meeting in Moscow as “counterproductive.” He says what’s needed is serious talks, and not a gimmick.

He also insists that Russia has no advantage on the frontline at the moment.

Merz then adds that the 19 November meeting will decide further support for Ukraine in 2027, and that concludes the press.

Meanwhile, two more false bomb threats were reported in Italy involving Ryanair flights from Lisbon and Dublin to Rome’s Fiumicino Airport, according to the Italian newspapers La Repubblica and Corriere della Sera.

The plane from Dublin was escorted by two Italian air force fighter jets, newspaper reported.

In both cases, routine checks were confirmed as negative, meaning no threat was identified, SkyTG24 said.

That brings the total of flights affected by false alarms today to at least six, up from four reported earlier (that we know of).

Asked about his expectations for the Abu Dhabi talks, Zelenskyy says he hopes to see future security guarantees for Ukraine to be covered, alongside a “prosperity package” that would help with the reconstruction of Ukraine.

He also says he hopes to discuss a proposal on energy, Black Sea truce, which is “in the interest of not only Ukraine, but also Russia and various nations in Africa.”

He reiterates that “there can be no stable peace unless Europe is at the negotiating table.”

Merz for his part says that “it is crucial for Russia to finally engage in serious negotiations.”

Responding, Zelenskyy thanks Germany for its continued help, including on air defence, and says that while many people will be following the events in the US today’s midterm elections, “the role of Europe – and specifically Germany – remains crucial.”

He says he briefed Merz on the latest attacks on Ukraine.

He also confirms the upcoming Abu Dhabi talks on 10-11 November (11:21), which he says hopefully “will pave the way for a trilateral meeting at the level of heads of states and government.”

He also confirms further talks on Ukraine will be held in Brussels on Wednesday (12:41), as per the European Commission’s announcement earlier, and that the coalition of the willing will meet in Berlin on 19 November.

Germany’s Merz begins by saying Zelenskyy’s visit comes “at a critical moment” as Russian attacks on Ukraine continued with “undiminished – indeed, increasing – severity and ruthlessness.”

He says Ukraine “can continue to rely on Germany,” with more support from Berlin in the coming months, including on air defence, energy, and broader winter-related aid.

Merz also warns that the outcome of the war in Ukraine “will determine European security for a long time to come.”

He says Germany will be coordinating with France and the UK, and will host a meeting of the Coalition of the Willing in Berlin later this month.

The German chancellor also talks of growing concerns about the broader effects of the continuing war, including a potential “global food crisis” triggered by Russia’s continued attacks on trading routes in the Black Sea.

He says the upcoming Abu Dhabi talks are “the right step forward,” and Europe hopes to play its part.

In the meantime, Berlin and partners will “increase the pressure on Moscow and continue to support Ukraine,” he says.

#111. Anthony Zurcher: Democrats push deep into Republican territory with historic wins

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

Sarah Smith on Democrats flipping the House of Representatives

This is already shaping up to be a historic night for Democrats, with the party winning control of US House of Representatives and on their way to flipping the Senate, too.

The Democrats notched their first shock win in the Senate, when Annie Andrews beat Darline Graham, who had been appointed to the seat after the death of her brother, Senator Lindsey Graham.

Few analysts had that seat in play even a few months ago.

Two Senate races that a year ago were thought to be battlegrounds have turned out to be slam dunks for the Democrats - Jon Ossoff's re-election in Georgia and the open Republican-held seat in North Carolina.

That North Carolina contest, where former Democratic Governor Roy Cooper bested Republican Party insider Michael Whatley, was the first of the four pick-ups Democrats need in order to wrest control of the Senate. With South Carolina in the bag, they are halfway home.

Attention has now turned to vote counts in Republican-held Senate seats in Texas, Kansas and Ohio.

The BBC's US partner CBS News is rating the Texas and Kansas Senate races as leaning Democrat. The governor's race in Texas, where just last year incumbent Republican Greg Abbott was a prohibitive favourite, is now considered a toss-up.

Meanwhile, the Democrats have won a House majority for the first time since 2022.

A look at the key races offers plenty of reasons why.

In Florida, Republicans in the state legislature had redrawn the boundaries of three Democratic-held seats to make it much easier to for them to win.

Other House races in the state, where Republicans expected to win by comfortable margins, have turned out to be dogfights.

Democrats flipped seats in western North Carolina, Pennsylvania and Kentucky, for instance, and appear in position to make gains in Virginia and Indiana.

These are the kinds of victories Democrats needed not only to win control of the chamber, but to give them a comfortable governing majority.

They're doing it on the back of high Democratic turnout, particularly by black voters in the south, and winning back Hispanic voters in Florida who had supported the Republicans just two years ago.

In majority black South Carolina counties, Democratic Senate candidate Andrews significantly outperformed Kamala Harris's presidential numbers in 2024.

And in Miami-Dade County, which Trump carried that year, Democratic candidates are back on top.

If there is a silver lining for conservatives, Florida as a whole still appears to tilt to the right.

Republican Senator Ashley Moody narrowly won re-election, as did congressman Byron Donalds in his bid for governor. But the Sunshine State may prove to be one of the few bright spots.

While the mood of the electorate appears to have turned against President Donald Trump and Republicans across the board, the US is a vast nation with unique geographic and demographic characteristics.

A good night for Democrats in the east does not necessarily translate to similar performance across the country. But early results from America's heartland seem to be following the east-coast trend.

A Democratic wave has formed on the horizon. Now it's just a question of exactly how far it crashes into Republican terrain.

#110. Iran Says It Would Reopen Strait of Hormuz in 2 Days as Part of U.S. Deal

Iranian officials spoke of some progress in negotiating to reopen the strait even as they prepared for all-out war. A Qatari delegation arrived in Tehran on Monday to meet with the foreign minister to continue talks.

#109. What we know about the alert app hack in Ukraine and Russia

Ukraine's emergency alert app issued a nationwide air raid warning, as well as alerts for a radiological threat, a chemical attack and street fighting in Kyiv on Sunday evening - all of which turned out to false.

A few hours later, Russia said its equivalent app had also issued false alerts, with both countries claiming their systems had been hacked.

It is still unclear who is to blame for the cyber-attacks and whether the two incidents are connected - the BBC's cyber correspondent Joe Tidy explains what we know so far.

#108. Spanish transport minister resigns after deadly train crash

Óscar Puente also announces his withdrawal from upcoming election following disaster that killed 69 people

Spain’s transport minister handed in his resignation on Tuesday after the train crash that killed 69 people last week, one of the worst railway disasters in Europe this century.

Óscar Puente, who had earlier pledged to work “tirelessly” to restore confidence in the world’s second-largest high-speed rail network, also said he was withdrawing from the country’s upcoming general election.

Last week’s crash, and another disaster earlier this year, have overshadowed campaigning for the 29 November ballot, in which the Socialist prime minister, Pedro Sánchez, is trailing in opinion polls.

Puente had ruled out resigning on several occasions since the disaster.

But at the end of a lengthy hearing before lawmakers, he said: “I wish to inform you that I am not only stepping down from my position as minister of transport, but that I am also withdrawing my name from the lists for the early legislative elections on 29 November.”

Hours earlier, at a press conference during which he presented measures to improve safety, the minister had said the government would “work tirelessly to achieve and regain the public’s trust in high-speed rail”.

The rightwing opposition, which had demanded Puente’s resignation, and unions had criticised him for appearing to pin all the blame for the disaster on one control centre operator.

The crash happened when a train operated by the state-owned company Renfe ploughed into the back of a stationary train belonging to the private Spanish operator Iryo on Wednesday outside the village of Urda, about 80 miles south of Madrid.

The failure of an automatic safety system halted all trains on a 39-mile stretch of track, but a control centre nonetheless authorised the Renfe service to resume its journey.

Among the measures announced at the earlier press conference by Puente was the creation of software for control centres that would issue automatic alerts and make it easier to spot anomalies in traffic.

Puente said an AI agent would be created to oversee the system and “minimise the possibility of human error”, describing the proposal as unprecedented.

In scenarios where a control centre operator took a decision while the automatic safety system was down, a second operator would have to confirm it, Puente added.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

The train drivers’ union Semaf, which has called strikes at Renfe for 10 and 12 November, has denounced a worsening of working conditions and the use of “degraded systems”.

Critics say investment in maintenance has not kept up with the increase in passenger numbers since the 2021 liberalisation of Spain’s rail network, which allowed private companies to break Renfe’s decades-long monopoly.

In an apparent recognition of the unions’ demands, Puente said the working conditions of control centre operators would be reformed, including their breaks and by monitoring their fatigue levels.

Puente also announced an aid package of €30m (£25m) for the families of the dead and the scores of people who were injured in the crash.

Another collision in the southern region of Andalusia in January killed 46 people on the same key line connecting Madrid with the city of Seville, Spain’s first high-speed route when it opened in 1992.
