#107. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

At least 501 children have been killed in Ukraine since February 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale invasion, said Catherine Russell, executive director of the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF).

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.

Russell warned the real figure is “likely far higher” than the numbers verified by the UN agency.

Additionally, almost 1,000 Ukrainian children have been injured, “leaving them with wounds and scars - both visible and invisible - that could last for life,” she added.

The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Russia’s independent news outlet Fontanka is reporting that the cafe belongs to Prigozhin, on the basis of the Concord company’s ownership mentioned on the cafe’s receipt.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

CNN has not been able to independently verify Prigozhin’s claims and whether he is the legal owner of the cafe.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#105. 'All this food is now useless': Piles of rubbish after shop freezers hit by cyber-attack

In dozens of supermarkets across several European countries, a huge amount of food has been dumped after refrigerators and freezers were targeted in a cyber-attack.

Belgium and Luxembourg saw some of the worst damage, with 40 Delhaize supermarkets affected, and incidents also took place in stores across Sweden, Ireland, the Netherlands, Estonia and Finland.

In a branch of supermarket Delhaize in Hoeilaart, just outside the Belgian capital Brussels, the BBC's Europe correspondent Nick Beake found frozen pizzas and ice cream among a mountain of food having to be scrapped. This branch estimated the cost of the damage running to more than â¬100,000 (Â£83,500).

#103. Canada joins coalition refusing to play ice hockey against Russia over Ukraine war

Growing number of countries are resisting Russia’s reintegration into international hockey after a sports court lifted a ban on competition

Canada has joined a coalition of countries refusing to play ⁠ice ​hockey teams from Russia, with prime minister Mark Carney saying Russian skaters will not be able to compete in ⁠a world junior championship next month due to the war in Ukraine.

“While Russia and Belarus are supporting this illegal, unjustified, horrific ‌war in Ukraine, as long as that’s going on, there will be none of ​those athletes competing for their countries in Canada,” Carney told reporters on Tuesday.

Carney’s comments follow announcements by a growing number of national hockey federations that they will not play Russian teams, after ‌a decision by the Court of Arbitration for Sport (CAS) to allow ‌Russians to compete in International Ice Hockey Federation (IHF) events.

An IIHF ban on Russian teams took ⁠effect after Moscow began its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022 and has been extended ​annually.

After several appeals by Russia’s hockey ‌federation, the CAS finally ‌lifted the ban on Friday, contending safety and security worries “were not supported by sufficient evidence”.

On Monday, the Finnish Ice Hockey Association asked other nations to stand up against the decision. Federations representing Denmark, Sweden, Norway, Latvia and the Czech Republic announced Tuesday they sided with Finland.

Ukraine’s foreign minister Andrii Sybiha on Tuesday thanked European nations for “refusing to share the ice with Russia”.

“If CAS lacks a moral compass, let’s show that hockey still has values,” he said in a post on X. “Refuse to play against Russia and deny visas to its players. Keep Russia off the ice! A war of aggression and hockey are incompatible.”

The Norwegians ‌were the first to announce on Tuesday their intention to play games in tournaments against all teams but those involving Russia.

“Our position that Russia should be excluded from all international championships as long as the war in Ukraine continues … remains unchanged,” Norway’s federation posted to social media.

“At this time, this decision is based on our responsibility to ‌protect the safety and wellbeing of our players, team staff and their families. We believe playing against Russia under the current circumstances could expose our players ​and team staff to significant security risks.”

Similar statements followed from the other three nations.

The CAS ruling has left cities worldwide to decide whether they will host upcoming tournaments, as planned, should Russians compete as the war continues in Ukraine.

The world junior ​tournament is scheduled to ​begin 26 December in Alberta.

#101. UK apologises to Ukrainian child refugees who were told to study Russian

Exclusive: Schools minister launches new Ukrainian GCSE and says she is sorry students were pushed to study Russian in its absence

The UK has apologised to Ukrainian child refugees who were told to study Russian in English schools, as it launched funding for a new GCSE in Ukrainian.

Ukrainian children who were forced to seek refuge in the UK after Vladimir Putin’s invasion in 2022 say it was “insulting” that their teachers suggested they study Russian in the absence of a GCSE in Ukrainian.

Announcing the new qualification, the schools minister, Georgia Gould, said: “So many students were pushed into having to do Russian. I’m really sorry that they were put in that position. It’s really important that they will have access to a Ukrainian GCSE.”

The new qualification, which will be ready in England in 2028, is being developed by the AQA exam board, with the help of a £340,000 grant from the Department for Education.

It comes after years of lobbying from the Ukrainian government and Ukrainian community groups on behalf of more than 30,000 Ukrainian children who have fled to the UK. In a letter to the DfE in 2024, the education ministry in Kyiv said: “We are deeply concerned that due to the lack of Ukrainian GCSE, many Ukrainian children are being pressured by their school staff to pass the Russian GCSE.”

Gould said: “It was one of the Ukrainian government’s asks of us that we deliver a Ukrainian GCSE, and one of the asks from the community and young people. We’re really proud to be able to do it, and proud of that partnership with the Ukrainian government.”

The Ukrainian language has been at the cultural frontline of the conflict with Russia. Putin has banned Ukrainian books and the teaching of Ukrainian in land Russia has occupied.

Gould said: “Whether it’s military support or this the kind of cultural support, our message as a British government is we stand absolutely steadfast behind the Ukrainian people.”

Ukraine’s education minister, Andrii Butenko, said he was grateful to the UK government. He said: “This will give Ukrainian children forced to leave their homes by Russia’s war of aggression against Ukraine an equal opportunity to choose their native language as a GCSE subject. We will continue working with AQA and the DfE to develop a high-quality and accessible qualification.”

Gould made the announcement on a visit to St Mary’s school in west London, the centre of a network of 13 Ukrainian-language schools in the UK. She was introduced to Vitalii Andronik, 20, who told the Guardian in 2024 how he had been urged to study Russian GCSE as a 15-year-old refugee. Gould told him: “I’m really sorry that you were put in that position.”

Andronik welcomed the new qualification and the apology. He said: “It’s too late to me, but I want future generations of refugees to study Ukrainian. It’s a recognition of the trauma that we went through. It’s brilliant news to hear that in future kids here won’t have to study Russian.”

Sofiia Chonka, 16, who fled to London from Zakarpattia in south-west Ukraine in June 2024 with her family, said: “I was told to study Russia in year 9, I just said ‘no’. I didn’t want anything to do with Russia – they killed my people and injured my family.”

Chonka, who has just started A-levels, said she would take the new exam when it was available. “It’s such a beautiful thing to be speaking Ukrainian when the Russians are trying to mute you.”

She added: “I’m really sad that it has taken so long, but at least they are starting the process, and some of my younger friends will have the opportunity to do it.

“It’s a stereotype that they are similar languages – they are completely different. Because Ukrainian is so beautiful, the Russians don’t like it. They can’t ban it, they can’t ban people.”

Inna Hryhorovych, the head of St Mary’s Trust, who has been calling for the GCSE since Russia’s annexation of Crimea in 2014, said: “It’s still hard to comprehend that we don’t need to fight for this any more.” She said her next campaign would be a new A-level in Ukrainian.

Gould said: “We’re just doing GCSEs for now. Let’s see how it goes. But we really want this to be a success.”

The minister said exams board had been nervous about developing a new qualification for a relatively small cohort of students. She said: “Despite the challenges of a smaller qualification, we really wanted to put financial resources to making it possible, and I’m grateful that AQA has come forward.

“I want this to happen as quickly as possible for people, but I’m really glad that it is happening now. Some of the students sitting here today will be able to study this GCSE.”

She encouraged British children to study for the new qualification as an act of solidarity. “I think it would be wonderful if more people were taking up Ukrainian GCSE,” she said, and she joked that Ukrainian children could tutor their friends as a “side hustle”.

When Gould announced the plan to a dozen Ukrainian pupils, they applauded and in unison said “diakuyemo”, or “thank you very much”.

#95. Canva layoffs: design giant to cut or relocate more than 1,200 jobs in major restructure

Chief executive Melanie Perkins describes job cuts as ‘without doubt the hardest moment in our thirteen-year history’ in email to staff

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Canva is cutting or relocating more than 1,200 jobs, representing 22% of its global workforce, as part of a major restructure that includes laying off staff who helped build the Australian-founded design platform.

The changes mirror a trend across the technology sector, where software companies are fighting to stay competitive against cheaper artificial intelligence disrupters while aggressively embedding AI tools themselves.

Canva’s chief executive, Melanie Perkins, said in an email to staff on Wednesday that the restructure was “without doubt the hardest moment in our thirteen-year history”.

“For some people, this will mean changes to their roles or being asked to relocate to centralised hubs,” she said. “Sadly, it will also mean saying goodbye to teammates and friends who have helped build Canva into what it is today.”

The restructure will affect 1,271 people, according to the company, as it focuses on “collapsing layers” and bringing “more teams together in person”.

“We know relocating is a big decision for those being asked to consider it, and we’re committed to making the process as smooth as possible with comprehensive support,” said Perkins, who did not specify the exact number of job losses.

Those leaving Canva will receive at least six months of salary and retain healthcare coverage for a further 12 months in countries where workers receive that benefit. They can also keep their laptops.

Those leaving can also apply for US$10,000 (A$14,000) grants to start their own ventures.

The graphic design company is one of Australia’s most successful startups and has long been touted as a candidate for a blockbuster listing on the Nasdaq.

But it has now added to a wave of restructures in the technology sector, which has included 1,600 redundancies at the software company Atlassian, and job losses at Afterpay’s owner, Block, and at WiseTech Global, another software company.

While established software companies have rejected the threat of a “SaaS-pocalypse”, a term used to describe the idea that AI becomes so advanced that specialised software becomes redundant, many have overhauled their business models in response or aggressively adopted AI tools.

Unlike Atlassian, Block and WiseTech, Canva did not mention AI in its restructure announcement.

#93. 'I'm not a quitter': Kim Kardashian on retaking the bar exam

Kim Kardashian has said she will retake the California bar exam with the aim of becoming a lawyer in real life, after playing one in the legal drama All's Fair.

She was speaking alongside co-star Naomi Watts ahead of the second series of All's Fair, which was roundly panned by critics despite breaking streaming records.

#91. Uber pays its drivers ‘wages’ and is liable for $81m payroll tax bill, Australia’s high court rules

Rideshare company unsuccessfully argued it didn’t employ workers and claimed money paid was not wages under NSW law

The rideshare behemoth Uber has suffered a bruising loss in Australia’s high court that will cost it more than $80m in unpaid taxes.

The court on Wednesday dismissed Uber’s claim it was not liable to pay payroll tax in New South Wales after it argued that it does not employ drivers and claimed that money paid to its drivers were not wages under state law.

In a six-to-one decision, the high court found that Uber is liable to pay $81m for the tax years from 2015 to 2020.

But the ruling is likely to leave the multinational with a significant bill for subsequent years, pushing the total well over $100m.

Uber was initially successful in appealing against the NSW chief commissioner of state revenue’s assessment but Wednesday’s ruling spells the end of the company’s legal avenues.

Six of seven justices agreed to dismiss the high court appeal, saying Uber’s drivers were employees and the money paid to them was wages.

“Uber has structured its rideshare system business so payments from riders are funnelled through it,” the majority said in the judgment.

“That gives Uber substantive control over the fares before they are remitted to drivers, allowing Uber to deduct its service fee, or change or cancel the fare entirely.”

Chief Justice Stephen Gageler noted that payroll tax laws had been adjusted late in the last century in multiple states to capture more revenue from contractual arrangements meant to disguise the true nature of the working relationship.

“The contractor provisions … have continued ‘always speaking in the present’ to participants in the gig economy which emerged in this century,” the chief justice wrote.

Last year the legal expert Celeste Black wrote that the case would probably influence the payroll tax obligations of companies with gig workers across Australia.

The NSW government said payroll tax was a key part of state’s tax base, despite only about one in 20 companies meeting the threshold to pay it.

“It helps fund essential services that families and households rely on,” the NSW finance minister, Courtney Houssos, said on Wednesday.

“Local businesses pay their fair share. Uber should pay their fair share too.”

#88. Supreme Court rejects Trump's request to stop California counting disputed votes

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

The US Supreme Court has rejected a last-minute request by the Trump administration asking it to intervene in California's midterm election vote count.

The justice department wanted 20,000 ballots to be set aside that it alleged may have been issued to non-US citizens in the state.

Two lower courts had already rejected the request. State election officials had questioned the federal government's claims - saying the process used to identify alleged non-citizens was inaccurate.

The Monday ruling comes on the eve of the midterms, which will determine which political party controls the US Congress for the last two years of President Donald Trump's second term.

The Supreme Court announced its decision in a one-line order, saying it was denying the justice department's request for an injunction.

"The application for injunction pending appeal presented to Justice Kagan and by her referred to the Court is denied," it reads.

Its means rulings made in lower courts will stand and essentially ends the Trump administration's bid to disqualify the ballots.

California is the most populous state in America with some 23 million people on its voting register. As of Sunday night, more than six million mail-in ballots had already been received, according to the California secretary of state's office.

The justice department sent letters to the California secretary of state and Los Angeles County Registrar last month, demanding they set aside the ballots of 20,000 registered voters whom the Trump administration "believe" are "likely" non-citizens, according to court documents.

About a week later, armed officials from the Department of Homeland Security delivered letters to county officials and employees across California threatening criminal charges if they didn't sequester those ballots.

In a 22-page brief reviewed by the BBC, the justice department maintained that ballots cast by non-citizens could "debase or dilute valid votes".

On Friday, a federal appeals court declined the government's request to segregate the ballots.

California election officials have questioned the accuracy of the government's claims, and have said the list of names the government has provided is riddled with inaccuracies.

They have expressed concern that eligible voters could be disenfranchised and said segregating ballots whilst voting is underway is unfeasible.

What are Americans voting for this election?

The attempts are a part of a years-long campaign by Trump and his administration to fight alleged voter fraud that they believe is widespread, despite research showing that it is extremely rare.

President Trump has repeatedly claimed that Democrats steal elections by allowing immigrants to vote illegally, and he blames much of it on the so-called "mail-in" or postal voting that is permitted in many Democratic states.

Last week in a scathing ruling, district judge David Carter said the government's move in California was indicative of an "ongoing pattern of attempted election interference" on the part of the Trump administration.

The judge added that "never before in American history has the federal government, on the eve of an election, sought to coerce a state and local government to abandon their own constitutional elections procedures and bend to the will of the executive branch".

With additional reporting by the BBC's David Willis

#87. Public service union refuses to back Labor at Victorian election, condemning job cuts and $1.2bn consultant spend

Exclusive: Community and Public Sector Union denounces ‘privatisation by stealth’, joining growing number of unions turning on Carroll government

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Victoria’s public servants union says it will not support Labor at this month’s state election, condemning the government for cutting public service jobs while spending $1.2bn on consultants.

The Community and Public Sector Union (CPSU) will announce the decision on Wednesday, joining several other unions that have refused to back Labor or are actively campaigning against the government ahead of the 28 November poll.

Although the CPSU’s Victorian branch is not formally affiliated with Labor, it has worked closely with the party in the past. But its secretary, Jiselle Hanna, told Guardian Australia it can no longer support the government.

“If Ben Carroll’s government won’t commit to no job cuts in the public service, then our union cannot support him,” Hanna said.

The Labor government has made successive cuts to the Victorian public service (VPS), a smaller subset of the public sector that is largely administrative, as it seeks to rein in employee expenses – the state’s largest area of expenditure.

This included cutting about 1,000 VPS roles including more than 300 executive positions, and merging several government entities following an independent review, led by Helen Silver, last year. The government has said the changes delivered $4bn in savings, while frontline roles weren’t affected.

As of June 2025, there were some 58,000 employed in the VPS – compared with the almost 400,000 in the total public sector.

Hanna said since the Silver review there had been more than 200 further restructures, many of which had resulted in “redundancies, job losses and pay cuts”.

“The public service used to be a reliable and secure place to build a career – that job security doesn’t exist any more, it’s been replaced with precarious employment,” she said.

Meanwhile, the Coalition has committed to implementing an indefinite hiring freeze across all back-office public service roles if it wins government, saving $22bn over a decade. The opposition has said the hiring freeze would remain in place until VPS staffing levels returned to the population-adjusted equivalent of 2014–15 – when it was last in government.

Hanna said both Labor and the Coalition’s distinction between frontline public sector roles and back-office VPS roles overlooked work performed by public servants including directing firefighters during bushfires; analysing DNA, fingerprints and ballistics; developing school curricula and investigating childcare centres.

She said while the union accepts “budget savings need to come from somewhere”, cuts to the public service would only lead to increased use of consultants, which she described as “privatisation by stealth”.

Analysis released by the union, based on Victorian government annual reports since the 2017-18 financial year, showed Labor has spent $1.2bn on consultants across 10 departments. The Department of Treasury and Finance had the highest spend of about $222m since 2017, while the Department of Energy, Environment and Climate Action had the highest annual spend of about $86m in the 2024-25 financial year.

The CPSU has launched a campaign urging Victorians to sign a pledge supporting the public service and committing not to vote for candidates who propose cutting jobs. While the campaign does not endorse any political party, it highlights that both the Greens and the Victorian Socialists oppose public service job cuts and the privatisation of public assets.

Last week, Guardian Australia reported the Electrical Trades Union (ETU) state council resolved not to run a statewide campaign for Labor, despite spending more than $1.6m supporting the party at the last election.

On the same day, the United Firefighters Union – which has been embroiled in a wages dispute with government since 2021 – launched its ad campaign urging Victorians to “put Labor last”.

The Australian Services Union is also running ads calling the premier a liar, as part of its long-term campaign to lift the 1.5% rate cap on councils, which restricts pay rises for workers below inflation.

Members of the Australian Education Union, which is not formally affiliated with the Labor party, have also said they won’t be campaigning for the party. The Health and Community Services Union, meanwhile, has eight members running this election – though none for the Labor party it is affiliated with.

Carroll told reporters on Wednesday he fights for workers “every single day”.

“I’ve been a member of the union movement since I was 14. I’ve been a member of the Labor party since I was 20,” he said.

#85. Trump to remove Rwanda and South Africa from US trade deal

Last year, Trump accused South Africa of white genocide in a meeting with President Cyril Ramaphosa at the White House

US President Donald Trump has notified Congress of his intention to remove South Africa and Rwanda from a preferential trade programme, from 1 January 2027.

The African Growth and Opportunity Act (Agoa) allows eligible African countries to export thousands of products to the US duty-free.

US Trade Representative Jamieson Greer said in a statement that an annual review had found that both countries were engaging in practices inconsistent with the programme's eligibility requirements.

He added that the Trump administration had additionally notified the two countries of the decision.

The Trump administration cited barriers to US trade and investment, as well as concerns over national security and foreign policy interests in the region.

For South Africa, the US pointed to corruption.

In a letter to South African President Cyril Ramaphosa, Trump said Pretoria's actions on international security and its relations and cooperation with governments of concern to Washington risked undermining US national security and foreign policy interests.

He also accused South Africa of maintaining significant barriers to American trade and investment, including restrictions affecting agricultural market access, regulatory requirements, investment protections and intellectual property.

"The Trump administration is working to ensure our African partners take the necessary steps to meet the eligibility criteria," Greer said.

South Africa's Trade Minister Parks Tau described the decision as "unfortunate", saying Washington was raising new issues that had not featured in trade negotiations between the two countries, which began in 2025.

"We will continue to accelerate the diversification of South Africa's export markets to protect jobs," Tau said.

South African white genocide does not exist, new ambassador to US tells BBC

Is there a genocide of white South Africans as Trump claims?

South Africa has also sought to put the potential impact of losing Agoa benefits into perspective, arguing that the country's broader trade relationship with the US extends well beyond the programme.

Government figures show that about 89% of South African exports to the US entered under ordinary World Trade Organization most-favoured-nation terms in 2025, while around 10% benefited from Agoa, down from 28% in 2024.

"The value of South African exports under the programme fell to about $1.7bn (Â£1.3bn) in 2025, from $4bn the previous year," Tau said.

Bilateral trade between the two countries reached $28.8bn in 2025, while more than 600 American companies operate in South Africa, supporting about 134,600 jobs.

Pretoria says the two economies have complementary trade interests. South Africa supplies 12 of the 50 minerals considered critical by the US, as well as 70% of American isotopes used, among other things, in cancer treatment.

South African manufacturers also use American inputs in products exported to the US under Agoa, including vehicles and boats.

Oxford Economics senior economist Jee-A van der Linde said Washington's move will put further pressure on South Africa's manufacturing sector, warning that affected exports may be difficult to redirect to other markets.

"This decision comes as global trade flows remained disrupted and South Africa faces growing competition from cheap Chinese imports, particularly in the automotive industry," he said.

However, he noted that South Africa had previously managed to redirect some tariff-affected exports to markets including Europe and China.

US Ambassador to South Africa Leo Brent Bozell III said it was regrettable that South African policies led to its termination from Agoa.

"This was a predictable and preventable outcome resulting from the rejection by South Africa's government of real concerns raised by the US," he wrote on X.

The announcement comes amid strained relations between Washington and both countries, with the proposed termination threatening their access to preferential trade arrangements with the US.

Last year, Trump accused South Africa of persecuting its white minority and launched a programme for white Afrikaners to get asylum in the US.

Trump also repeated widely discredited claims of a genocide of South Africa's white population.

In Rwanda's case, Washington also raised concerns about human rights, political pluralism, the rule of law and workers' rights.

The move comes barely a week after Rwanda's defence ministry was hit with sanctions by US Treasury for its role in the armed rebellion in eastern DR Congo.

The US Treasury said Rwanda's defence ministry directed thousands of troops to fight alongside M23 rebels in eastern DR Congo, providing weapons, equipment, logistics and training.

Rwanda has denied backing the M23 despite overwhelming evidence.

Rwanda saw its exports to the US rise to $43.3 million in 2025, up from $13.1 million in 2024, according to US import data.

Alexander Venter, an Oxford Economics economist, said Washington accounted for about 2% of Rwanda's merchandise exports in 2025, with only around 4% of its exports to the US benefiting from Agoa preferences.

Neighbouring African countries and Asian markets continued to dominate Rwanda's trade, Venter added.

#80. Jet may have broken apart before Florida crash, officials say, as family named among victims

Dashcam footage captures Florida private jet crash

Investigators believe parts of a private jet that crashed in Florida and killed all nine onboard may have broken off while it was in the air.

The Learjet 60 had been bound for the Bahamas from Austin, Texas when it nosedived into a multi-lane highway and exploded on impact.

A tech entrepreneur and his young family, as well as the 20-year-old co-pilot, were among those who died on Sunday.

The US National Transportation Safety Board (NTSB) said weather conditions would be a major focus of the investigation but that it was struggling to access the wreckage due to the huge crater created by the jet on the road in Palm Beach.

The plane crashed on Military Trail, a six-lane road that features businesses and restaurants, at around 12:50 local time (17:50 GMT).

"The biggest obstacle that we're going to have is recovering the major wreckage from the impacted crater on the roadway," lead investigator Tim Monville said at a news conference on Monday.

The NTSB said the crater, near the entrance to a mobile home park, had become partially filled with water because of severe storms. Heavy rainfall and thunderstorms had been reported in the area at the time of the crash.

Debris was scattered across sports fields, businesses and homes in the area.

"We do have some evidence that there was some breakup of the aircraft before impact," the NTSB's Tom Chapman said.

"We may find parts of the aircraft some distance away from the actual impact site."

Officials are also trying to find out whether a cockpit voice recorder was onboard at the time of the crash and whether it can be recovered.

Flight analysis indicates that the jet was plummeting hundreds of feet per second before it crashed.

In air traffic control audio recordings, someone in the cockpit can be heard reporting "moderate turbulence" and that the crew was "managing the ice".

They can then be heard saying the plane was "picking up precipitation" and "we need to climb". Moments later, the same voice said the plane was descending at a rate of 10,000ft (3,048m) per minute.

An emergency was declared and they can be heard yelling "we are in an overspeed condition descending 20,000ft per minute", before saying, "we are going to crash into the ground".

Seven passengers died on board, named in a joint statement as tech entrepreneur Adam Robinson, his wife Helen Sharp, and their two children - Emma, four, and Charlie, two - as well as friends of the family Rachael Reed and Charmi Fehr, and nanny Anita Maria Cetina.

The pilot was named in media reports as 55-year-old Jeffrey Gregory, while Nathalia Filgueiras, 20, who earned her pilot's licence when she was 17, was reported to be the co-pilot.

One man in a car on the ground sustained a leg injury.

Tech entrepreneur Adam Robinson and his wife Helen Sharp with their two small children, Emma and Charlie

Robinson and Sharp's family described them as "devoted parents" and said their children were "the light of their lives".

"Helen and Adam were the love affair that will last forever. Their warmth, generosity and gift for making people feel welcome touched so many lives," the statement read.

Robinson described himself on his LinkedIn page as the founder and CEO of RB2B and Retention.com, and the CEO of Moltsets - all technology companies. Helen Sharp was an artist, according to media reports.

Robinson previously worked at the global financial services business Lehman Brothers and the banking group Barclays. The BBC has contacted the companies.

Anita Maria Cetina, Charmi Fehr and Rachel Reed

The joint statement also named nanny Anita Maria Cetina and said she "cared for Emma and Charlie with tenderness and devotion, and she was truly part of the family".

It added: "She showed us a legacy of unconditional love and devotion."

Rachel Reed and Charmi Fehr were described in the joint family statement as "full of joy and adventure".

"They loved their people fiercely, fully and without hesitation. From the moment they met, they were all in on each other and the life they were building."

Nathalia Filgueiras received her pilot's licence when she was in high school

Nathalia Filgueiras's mother, Camila Corteleti, confirmed to US media that her daughter had died in the crash.

Corteleti told the New York Times: "She loved the freedom. It was completely different seeing the world from the top."

Filgueiras had spoken of her pride at receiving her pilot's licence while still a senior in high school in an interview with a local affiliate of CBS News in 2023.

"I like being different than everyone," she said. "Some people think that it's weird that I can fly planes but I can't drive a car - I mean, I can, but a lot of people think it's funny."

A spokesperson for East Coast Jets, where pilot Jeff Gregory used to work, said: "We are deeply saddened to learn that Capt Jeff Gregory was among those who lost their lives in yesterday's tragic aviation accident in Palm Beach."

He was married with two children, US media report.

#79. Alleged attack on One Nation supporter not believed to be politically motivated, Victoria police say

Police also allege Torquay man was attacked with a ‘blunt weapon’ rather than a baseball bat as originally reported

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

An alleged attack on a One Nation supporter is not believed to have been politically motivated, Victoria police say, clarifying that a “blunt weapon” was allegedly used rather than a baseball bat as originally reported.

On Tuesday, police confirmed they arrested a 37-year-old Hampton Park man and a 14-year-old boy over the alleged attack on a 60-year-old man outside his Torquay home on Sunday night.

“It is believed they allegedly assaulted the man with a baseball bat before being arrested by attending units a short time later,” police said on Tuesday.

But on Wednesday, police had removed references to the baseball bat from their statement and instead alleged a “blunt weapon” was used.

“Detectives are not treating the incident as politically motivated,” a spokesperson said.

Police are believed to be treating the incident as a random attack.

Prior to the alleged attack on Sunday, police intercepted a vehicle on Blackgate Road – a few kilometres from the victim’s Torquay home. The driver, a 37-year-old man from Hampton Park, allegedly returned a positive breath test and his vehicle was impounded. He is expected to be charged on summons.

Police said the man smashed the windows of his own vehicle during the incident.

The Hampton Park man was charged with intentionally causing injury and other assault-related offences, and was remanded in custody to appear again on 10 November, while the boy was issued with a caution.

Leaders from across the political spectrum condemned the alleged attack, with One Nation’s federal leader, Pauline Hanson, visiting the alleged victim in Geelong hospital on Tuesday and the party launching an online fundraiser, which was later shared on X by Elon Musk. As of 1pm Wednesday, it had raised more than $250,000.

One Nation said on Wednesday it would not comment.

On Tuesday, Hanson alleged the victim had been “kicked [so] badly he lost consciousness”. He was left “bleeding from basically every orifice in his face”, as well as bleeding in the brain, she told reporters outside the hospital on Tuesday.

“It was a vicious attack. It wasn’t just a punch,” Hanson alleged. “Even now, with the bleed, it still could be life-threatening.”

The party’s Victorian leader, Warren Pickering, said on Tuesday the victim was a volunteer and alleged the incident began when the man and the boy “interfered with” the One Nation signs on his property.

“Political violence or intimidation of any kind against anyone is unacceptable in this country,” Pickering said.

“If you don’t like someone’s politics, you vote accordingly. You don’t [allegedly] assault them.”

Speaking on ABC Radio Melbourne on Wednesday morning, the Victorian premier, Ben Carroll reiterated his condemnation of the alleged attack, describing it as “abhorrent” and “disgusting”.

“This man putting up corflutes is entitled to do that. Every Victorian, no matter whether they’re wearing an orange, blue, red – or green for that matter – T-shirt, is entitled to put their case forward in a civil matter, in a civil way,” Carroll said.

#76. Russian bombing kills 31 people at Ukraine aid centre, Kyiv says

Video shows smoke rising from Komyshuvakha blast crater

At least 31 people have been killed after Russian bombs hit an aid distribution centre in Zaporizhia, the Ukrainian regional head has said, describing it as one of the deadliest attacks there since the start of the full-scale invasion.

Mykhailo Semikin said at least nine 3,000kg aerial bombs hit the village of Komyshuvakha in southern Ukraine on Monday and the aid centre collapsed from a "direct hit".

"They had come for help - not to a military facility, not to a frontline position, but to a place where they were supposed to receive support," he wrote on Telegram.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky called the attack "utterly horrific" and said there were still people "under the rubble".

Zelensky posted a video showing a huge crater left after Monday's attack and rescuers searching for survivors.

However, officials say teams have at times been forced to halt their search because of Russian drone attacks in the area.

Zelensky said after the first strike on the local school "used for humanitarian needs", there was a second attack on the place where people were sheltering.

He earlier said that "the Russians could not have been unaware that the local residents who remained in Komyshuvakha received humanitarian aid there every week" on Monday.

The Russian military has not publicly commented.

There are fears that the death toll will rise further as a number of people are still under the rubble

Three days of mourning have been declared in the region.

The death toll has continued to rise on Tuesday. Earlier, when 29 people were confirmed to have died, Semikin said another 10 had been injured and 48 were reported missing.

Among the dead are two rescuers and four police officers, according to Ukraine's state emergencies service DSNS.

Zelensky said the attack was "yet another proof that Russia does not want any de-escalation".

He said that after a recent successful joint Russian-Ukrainian operation to evacuate civilians from the Russia-occupied southern city of Oleshky during a temporary ceasefire brokered by US First Lady Melania Trump, "there was a moment when the next steps could show that peace is possible".

"With such killings, Russia is sending the exact opposite signal - Russia is counting on further killings," Zelensky said.

The Russian strikes left a huge crater where Ukrainian officials say people were sheltering

Russian President Vladimir Putin launched a full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022.

Teams from Ukraine, Russia and the US are due to resume peace talks in Abu Dhabi, the United Arab Emirates, next week, US President Donald Trump's special envoy Steve Witkoff said on X.

Since the talks were suspended in February, Moscow has stepped up attacks as Kyiv virtually ran out of Western-supplied interceptors to defend against missiles.

No major breakthrough is expected at the talks. Witkoff said the discussions would focus on "post-war security arrangements, de-escalation, humanitarian efforts, and next steps toward peace".

Witkoff said US negotiators would also meet European and Nato officials this week in Brussels.

#72. Thousands of European troops to rehearse Ukraine deployment in event of peace

A British paratrooper takes part in a previous Nato training exercise less than 50km (31 miles) from the Russian border in Finland

Britain and France are to lead a military exercise to test a multinational force's ability to deploy to Ukraine, if there is a peace deal.

Nearly 3,000 military personnel from 12 European nations will take part in the rehearsal in neighbouring Poland this month.

Plans for a reassurance force for Ukraine were set out last year as one of the long-term security guarantees being proposed by European nations.

But there are still more questions than answers as to how such a military force might work in practice. Western officials stress that any force sent to Ukraine would not take part in offensive operations or be involved in peacekeeping or ceasefire monitoring missions.

Nor would they be operating under the command of Nato or on the front line.

Officials say their presence would be to provide reassurance to Ukraine and to help rebuild its armed forces.

The US has been involved in discussions, but is not contributing to the mission.

Russia has already voiced opposition to foreign troops being stationed in Ukraine and, last year, President Vladimir Putin said they would be "legitimate targets".

Western officials have declined to spell out the rules of engagement if any international troops were to be attacked in Ukraine. Though they say the force would be able to defend itself.

UK Defence Secretary Wes Streeting said the exercise showed Britain, France and their allies were stepping up preparation for the day when peace came to Ukraine.

He said: "This is not a hypothetical plan, it's a real force, ready to help Ukraine once the fighting stops."

Lt Gen Tom Bateman, the UK commander of the Multi National Force â Ukraine (MNF-U), said the exercise represented an important step in translating political commitments into credible military readiness.

He said it was a "real, capable, readily deployable force that represented a serious commitment from Coalition of the Willing nations to Ukraine's long-term stability and security".

Ukrainian troops take part in urban warfare training at a military base in England in 2023

Exercise Koalicja - which takes place from 9-22 November - will be a rehearsal of how to move, protect and supply a force in Ukraine. It will also be a test of their command and control to ensure troops from different countries can work together.

Some 2,800 military personnel will take part across land, sea and air, with the UK contribution including 300 troops and a pair of RAF Typhoons.

Fighter jets will simulate how they would reassure commercial aircraft once Ukraine's skies can be reopened.

Turkey has agreed to lead the maritime mission, with the eventual goal to clear mines and allow the resumption of trade in the Black Sea.

As well as the UK, troops will come from France, Belgium, Estonia, Germany, Lithuania, the Netherlands, Poland, Spain, Sweden, and Turkey.

However, Western officials could not comment on the final make-up and shape of the force that could be deployed to Ukraine if the fighting stops.

In the past, military experts have suggested that Western nations might have to send tens of thousands of troops to Ukraine to guarantee its security.

Officials acknowledge that the countries' commitment and contributions will depend on the circumstances of a cessation of hostilities and individual nations' political decisions.

It is also unclear what training role Western forces could provide to battle hardened Ukraine.

Ukrainian soldiers are already in high demand training Nato forces - passing on their expertise in drone warfare.

Above all, Russia has shown no sign of wanting to end its war.

#69. Asian Cup postponed in wake of attacks on Saudi Arabia

Aston Villa will have goalkeeper Zion Suzuki more available this season because of the rescheduling of the Asian Cup

The 2027 Asian Cup in Saudi Arabia has been postponed because of a series of deadly attacks on the host country.

The 24-team tournament was scheduled to take place from 7 January to 5 February but has been rescheduled to run from 23 December, 2027 to 21 January, 2028.

In October, a missile attack by Yemen's Houthi movement on Riyadh's airport killed 13 people and wounded more than 300.

A statement from the the Asian Football Confederation (AFC), external said the decision to reschedule had been taken "in light of the current exceptional circumstances arising from developments in the region, including substantial logistical complexities and travel disruptions".

It added: "The new dates of the tournament will be presented to Fifa for approval."

The Saudi Pro League has postponed this week's round of fixtures following the rescheduling of the Asian Cup.

It says the rescheduling creates "additional flexibility" in the calendar to allow for a "more balanced distribution of fixtures" across the rest of the season.

A number of events in Saudi Arabia have been cancelled recently, including the Six Kings Slam tennis exhibition event, which was scheduled to be in Riyadh.

The Asian Tour has also indefinitely postponed it's season-ending golf tournament, which was scheduled to take place from 18-21 November.

This is the second successive Asian Cup to be postponed after the 2023 tournament was moved to 2024 and to Qatar from China because of the Covid pandemic.

Saudi minister vows 'serious response' to Houthi attacks on airports

The decision to postpone the 2027 Asian Cup means a number of Premier League players will not have to miss part of the current season.

Some of the players that would have been expected to be called up were Japan's Zion Suzuki of Aston Villa, Wataru Endo, who plays for Liverpool, and Daizen Maeda of Ipswich Town.

Leeds United's Ao Tanaka, Crystal Palace duo Takehiro Tomiyasu and Daichi Kamada, and Hull City's Hidemasa Morita also play for Japan.

Japan would have been one of the favourites for the tournament and Villa, who are in the Champions League, might have had to do without Suzuki for at least seven games if his country had reached the final.

Liverpool are also in the Champions League but Endo has yet to play for the Reds this season.

Brighton winger Kaoru Mitoma is currently injured but is expected to be back later this year and is also likely to have been called up by Japan.

Manchester City defender Abdukodir Khusanov, of Uzbekistan, and Hull City and Australia defender Lucas Herrington would also have expected international call-ups for the Asian Cup.

#67. Aukus is ‘worst, riskiest and most costly’ Australian defence decision ever, public inquiry into submarine deal says

Report led by Peter Garrett and former defence chief Adm Chris Barrie says agreement should be suspended and calls for royal commission

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

A public inquiry into the $368bn Aukus nuclear submarine agreement has called the plan “the worst, riskiest and most costly defence decision ever made by an Australian government”, urging its immediate termination.

Former Labor MP and Midnight Oil frontman Peter Garrett and former chief of defence Adm Chris Barrie led the inquiry dominated by critics of the deal with the UK and US, recommending the suspension of plans for new bases and for a royal commission to be held into the agreement signed by the former Morrison government.

Its report, titled Surrendering Australia: the Aukus Deception, says Australia risks being dragged into military conflict with China because of the presence of powerful nuclear submarines. Aukus badly undermines Australia’s sovereignty and undercuts longstanding commitments to nuclear non-proliferation, it says.

The report calls for a rework of the second pillar of the agreement, which covers technology and defence capability sharing, and for a fundamental recalibration of Australia’s relationship with Beijing.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

After hearings around the country and more than 1,000 public submissions, the inquiry highlighted a lack of scrutiny and oversight over the five years since Aukus was announced and called for the auditor general and federal parliament to launch independent reviews.

“Since coming to power, Labor has failed to conduct a thorough review of this most significant of defence agreements,” Garrett told the National Press Club in Canberra.

“Conceived in secret, Aukus has spurned established defence and parliamentary oversight practices.”

He said the Stirling defence precinct in Perth risked becoming a “de facto US base” hosting as many as 2,000 serving American personnel, and that nearby communities including Rockingham and Fremantle would become targets for attack.

“The Aukus submarine plan is not tenable,” Barrie, who served as chief of defence between 1998 and 2002, said.

“It’s time for our government to take the lead on this matter. Scrap the Aukus submarine plan now, and rebuild national security decision-making processes.”

Labor agreed to support the deal for Australia to acquire nuclear submarines, which was negotiated under Morrison, then US president Joe Biden and UK prime minister Boris Johnson. As part of the agreement, Australia is funding upgrades to the US defence industrial base and is due to start receiving secondhand Virginia class nuclear submarines around 2032.

But delays and cost blowouts for production of submarines for the US Navy have led to scepticism that Australia will receive the in-service boats.

The UK parliament held a year-long review and, after an inquiry by the Pentagon in 2025, Donald Trump agreed to support Aukus.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

But former prime ministers Paul Keating and Malcolm Turnbull, former foreign affairs ministers Gareth Evans and Bob Carr, environmental and security campaigners, and some within Labor believe Aukus is not in Australia’s national interest.

Garrett warned any certainty the US president would eventually agree to send submarines to Australia could not be guaranteed.

“There are no refunds, no enforceable obligations on partner countries, and no recourse if things go wrong,” he said.

The inquiry estimates an additional $180bn will be needed to deal with nuclear waste because of Aukus.

Australia has not identified a permanent storage site for the nuclear waste generated by the submarine fleet, including the high-level radioactive waste from the reactor core and spent fuel, which will remain toxic for thousands of years.

The government’s preferred measure of the total cost of the deal is 0.15% of GDP over its lifetime.

The first Virginia-class nuclear sub from the US is due to arrive in Australia in 2032, with another arriving every four years, before the Australian-built model is ready for operations. The bespoke SSN Aukus model is due to come online in 2042.

Yet as more and more patients seek medical advice, getting an appointment with a dermatologist is becoming increasingly difficult. Half the country's dermatologists are nearing retirement, and only around 100 new specialists are trained up each year. Faced with this growing shortage, some are coming up with innovative solutions. FRANCE 24's Aurore Dupuis and Sonia Baritello report.

#65. Mining giant joins fight to stop killer toad march across Australia

Rio Tinto is ploughing millions of dollars into stopping the poisonous cane toad reaching the Pilbara region of western Australia, where the mining giant has a vast iron ore operation.

The firm, alongside Curtin University in Perth and others, will spend A$28m (Â£14.5m) building a "toad containment zone" to prevent the pest travelling south.

While the cane toad is not a direct threat to Rio Tinto's business, it produces toxins lethal to native wildlife and poses a threat to Pilbara's ecosystem.

Rio Tinto has previously faced criticism for destroying two sacred Aboriginal rock shelters in Pilbara which sat above tonnes of iron ore.

The decision cost the former chief executive his job, provoked a widespread backlash and saw Rio Tinto commit to rebuilding trust with Indigenous communities.

Commenting on the proposed containment zone, Roberta Hunter, a ranger in Australia's Nyangumarta Warrarn Indigenous Protected Area, said: "Cane toads destroy the water and animals.

"When toads come close to our country, we want to be there to stop them because we don't want the country to be destroyed."

The plan is to cut off the toad's water supply by building a barrier across 7,500 sq km (2,895 sq m) of land between the Great Sandy Desert and the Indian Ocean.

In drier months, the cane toad needs water every few days to survive.

If there are no natural water sources, the toad relies on artificial water points normally used for cattle and irrigation.

The funding will be used to stop the toad accessing dams, storage tanks or other ground-level water sources. The five-year project will begin in 2027.

A number of native species are at risk if the toad gets into Pilbara. These include the northern quoll, whose numbers have been decreasing, the goanna lizard and the ghost bat.

The toads were introduced into Australia in 1935 to control beetles within the sugar cane industry.

The northern quoll is at risk from the cane toad in Australia

Since then, they have been on a slow march west across the country's northern landscape, according to the Australian government, travelling between 40 km and 60 km a year.

There are no predators or diseases that control the toads, which defend themselves using poison.

Ben Phillips, a professor at Curtin University, said the toads would reach Pilbara within two decades without action.

He said: "The toad containment zone is a once in a lifetime opportunity to stop one of the world's worst invasive species in its tracks and protect the region's unique wildlife."
